\displaystyle c_{2}^{-1}n^{-1}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c_{2}^{-1}\Lambda^{-2}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}.

Now we use the same expressions as we derived in Section
E.2
to write

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}+\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon_{q}({\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-2}({\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{2}^{-1}\Diamond^{2}/2+2\varepsilon_{q}({\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-2}({\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}).

Note that conditionally on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
with probability
0.5
over the draw of
\varepsilon_{q}
the term involving
\varepsilon_{q}
is non-negative, that is
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\|\geq(2c_{2})^{-1/2}\Diamond
. That statement doesn’t involve
{\boldsymbol{y}}
, so
{\boldsymbol{y}}
is still independent of this event. Thus, conditionally on it, by Lemma
41
for an absolute constant
c_{y,2}
with probability at least
c_{y,2}^{-1}
over the choice of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
we have
|{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}|\geq c_{y,2}^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\|
. Moreover, we’ve seen in the first part of the proof that with probability at least
1-c_{y,1}e^{-n/c_{y,1}}
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}

|{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}|\leq c_{1}\sqrt{n}\Diamond
. Finally, with probability
0.5
over
\varepsilon_{y}
we have
\varepsilon_{y}{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}=-|{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}|
. Combining everything together (recall that
\varepsilon_{q},\varepsilon_{y},{\boldsymbol{Q}}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
are independent) we get that on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
with probability at least
0.25\left(c_{y,2}^{-1}-c_{y,1}e^{-n/c_{y,1}}\right)
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
and
(\varepsilon_{q},\varepsilon_{y})

\displaystyle\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\leq

\displaystyle-c_{y,2}^{-1}(2c_{2})^{-1/2}\Diamond,

\displaystyle\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\leq

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-1}M(c_{1}^{2}+2c_{1})-c_{y,2}^{-1}(2c_{2})^{-1/2}\Diamond

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 0,

where the last transition holds for
a
large enough depending on
c_{1},c_{2},c_{y,1},c_{y,2}
since
n\Lambda^{-1}M<a^{-1}\Diamond
.
∎

H.2
Denominator

The next step is to lower-bound the denominator
\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
. Recall that
\varepsilon_{y},\varepsilon_{q},{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{Q}}
are all independent from each other. We factor out the randomness in each of those variables one-by-one, starting with the following

Lemma 51
.

With probability at least
0.25
over the choice of
(\varepsilon_{y},\varepsilon_{q})
(that is, conditionally on
{\boldsymbol{y}}
and
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
)

\displaystyle\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}({{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\left(\left\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+0.5\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{7}{2}({{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 7(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

Proof.

Note that for any vectors
{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{v}}
of the same dimension the following holds:

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{u}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}+2{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}-2\left(0.25\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}+4\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.5\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}-7\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}.

Thus, we write

\displaystyle\|\bar{S}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle 0.5\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}+\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}-7(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}.

For the last term note that

\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}=\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}=\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon_{y}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}=\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}.

Next, we decompose the first term as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}+\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}+({{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}+2\varepsilon_{y}f_{1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{y}}),

where
f_{1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{y}})
is a cross-term, which doesn’t involve
\varepsilon_{y}
. Recall that for the first term we have
\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}=\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}
.

For the second term in that decomposition we go a step further and write

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}-\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{q}f({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{q}f_{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+0.5\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}-7\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{q}f_{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}).

where
f_{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
is the cross term, which is independent from
\varepsilon_{q}
,
\varepsilon_{y}
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
.

The statement of the lemma holds on the following event

\{\varepsilon_{q}f_{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\geq 0,\varepsilon_{y}f_{1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},{\boldsymbol{Q}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\varepsilon_{q}{\boldsymbol{y}})\geq 0,\}

whose probability is at least
0.25
conditionally on
{\boldsymbol{y}},{\boldsymbol{Q}}
since
\varepsilon_{q}
and
\varepsilon_{y}
are independent random signs.
∎

Note that the last term in the lemma above still depends on
\varepsilon_{q}
through
\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}
and
\bar{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}
. This is not a problem since we will bound that term almost surely over the draw of
\varepsilon_{q}
conditionally on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
and
{\boldsymbol{y}}
.

The next step is to obtain lower bounds w.r.t. randomness that comes from
{\boldsymbol{y}}
. Once again, we don’t touch the terms that we subtract yet, and only lower-bound the positive terms.

Lemma 52
.

There exists an absolute constant
c_{y}
such that for any fixed value of
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
for any
t\in(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{y})
with probability at least
c_{y}^{-1}-c_{y}e^{-t^{2}/c_{y}}
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
all the following hold almost surely over the draw of
\varepsilon_{q}
:

\displaystyle\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c_{y}^{-1}\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\geq

\displaystyle(n-k)\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-1}-c_{y}(t\sqrt{n}+t^{2})\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\|,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq

\displaystyle n\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\|,

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle c_{y}(\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})+t^{2}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\|),

\displaystyle|\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}|\leq

\displaystyle c_{y}t\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\|.

Proof.

The first inequality is a direct application of Lemma
42
, and the remaining were shown as a part of Lemma
43
. Note that only the last inequality depends on
\varepsilon_{q}
, and formally Lemma
43
only shows it for a fixed value of
\varepsilon_{q}
. However, there are only two possible values of
\varepsilon_{q}
, so the uniform result can be obtained by straightforward multiplicity correction (the constant from Lemma
43
should be doubled).
∎

At this point we just need the lower bounds on the quantities that only involve
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. These are done by the following

Lemma 53
.

Suppose that the distribution of the rows of
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
is
\sigma_{x}
-sub-Gaussian.
For any
L\geq 1
there exists a constant
c
that only depends on
L,c_{B}
and
\sigma_{x}
such that the following holds. Suppose that
k<n/c
and
{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}
is independent from
{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}
. There exists an event
\mathscr{C}
whose probability is at least
1-ce^{-n/c}
such that all the following hold on the event
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
:

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})\geq

\displaystyle c^{-1}V,

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c^{-1}\Lambda^{2}n^{-2}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

We prove the inequalities separately:

1.

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})=

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})+\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})

For the first term we use the same formula as in Section
E.5
:

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{tr}\left({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-2}\mu_{k}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})\text{tr}\left(\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\right)^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{n}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{2}\mu_{k}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})}{\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{2}\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})^{2}}\text{tr}\left(\left(\mu_{k}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})^{-1}\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle L^{4}c_{B}^{3}n^{-1}\cdot c_{B}^{2}L^{2}\text{tr}\left(\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right),

where we used Lemma
46
in the penultimate line and the definition of the event
\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
from Section
3.2
in the last transition.

When it comes to the second term, we once again (as in the proof of Lemma
53
) are going to use the fact that

{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}={\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}.

Thus, we write

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-2}\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-2}\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-2}\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-2}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i},

where
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}
are columns of
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
. Note that for every fixed
i>k
the vector
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}
has i.i.d. components with variance
1
and sub-Gaussian constant at most
\sigma_{x}
which are independent of
{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}
. As in the proof of Lemma
53
, by Hanson-Wright inequality (Lemma
37
) for some absolute constant
c_{1}
and any
s>0
with probability at least
1-2e^{-s/c_{1}}

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}-\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp})|=

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}-(n-k)|

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}^{2}\max(\sqrt{s}\|{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}\|_{F},s\|{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}\|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}^{2}\max(\sqrt{s}\sqrt{n-k},s).

So, for a large enough constant
c_{2}
that only depends on
\sigma_{x}
, given that
c>c_{2}
(i.e.,
k<n/c_{2}
) for any separate
i
with probability at least
1-c_{2}e^{-n/c_{2}}

{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\geq n/c_{2}.

By Lemma 9 from
Bartlett et al. (2020)
, we can combine separate high-probability lower bounds on non-negative terms into a high-probability lower bound on the sum, that is, with probability at least
1-2c_{2}e^{-n/c_{2}}

\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0:k}^{\perp}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\geq\frac{n}{2c_{2}}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

Take this event as
\mathscr{C}
.

Overall, we get that on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{C}

\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})\geq\frac{1}{2L^{2}c_{2}}\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

2.

Using Lemma
45
we have

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{1/2}\left({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})^{-2}\mu_{n}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{2}\left\|\left(\mu_{k}({\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}_{0:k})^{-1}\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle L^{-2}c_{B}^{-2}\Lambda^{2}n^{-2}\cdot c_{B}^{-2}L^{-2}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2},

where we used Lemma
46
in the penultimate line and the definition of the event
\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
from Section
3.2
in the last transition.

∎

Finally, we can put everything together in the following

Lemma 54
.

Suppose that the distribution of the rows of
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
is
\sigma_{x}
-sub-Gaussian.
Take some
L>1
. There is an absolute constant
c_{y}
and a constant
c
that only depends on
L
and
\sigma_{x}
, such that if
k<n/c
and

\Lambda>c\left(n\lambda_{k+1}+\sqrt{n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}}\right),

(55)

then there exists an event
\mathscr{C}
which only depends on
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
, whose probability is at least
1-ce^{-n/c}
such that conditionally on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
with probability at least
c_{y}^{-1}-c_{y}e^{-n/c}
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
with probability at least
0.25
over the draw of
(\varepsilon_{y},\varepsilon_{q})

\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\geq c^{-1}(V+n\Diamond^{2}).

Proof.

Take the event
\mathscr{C}
to be the same as in Lemma
53
, and denote the constant from it as
c_{1}
. By that lemma on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})\geq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{-1}V,

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c_{1}^{-1}\Lambda^{2}n^{-2}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}.

Moreover, denote the constant from Lemma
47
as
c_{2}
. Note that
k<n/c_{2}
and
\Lambda>c_{2}n\lambda_{k+1}\vee\sqrt{n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}}
on if
c
is large enough. Thus, we by Lemma
47
on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\|\leq

\displaystyle c_{2}\Diamond,

\displaystyle\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1})\leq

\displaystyle c_{2}V

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\|\leq

\displaystyle c_{2}\Delta V.

Now denote the constant from Lemma
52
as
c_{y}
. Combining that lemma with the results we stated above we get that conditionally on the event
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
for any
t\in(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{y})
with probability at least
1-c_{y}e^{-t^{2}/c_{y}}
all the following hold almost surely over the draw of
\varepsilon_{q}
:

\displaystyle\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c_{y}^{-1}c_{1}^{-1}V,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\geq

\displaystyle(n-k)\mu_{1}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{k})^{-1}-c_{y}(t\sqrt{n}+t^{2})\|{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-1}(L^{-1}(1-k/n)-c_{y}L\Lambda^{-1}(t\sqrt{n}+t^{2}),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\leq

\displaystyle nL\Lambda^{-1},

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle c_{y}c_{2}(V+t^{2}\Delta V),

\displaystyle|\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}|\leq

\displaystyle c_{y}c_{2}t\Diamond.

For the second inequality let’s restrict
t
to the range
(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{3})
, where
c_{3}
is a large enough constant depending on
\sigma_{x},L
, so that inequality implies
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}\geq c_{3}^{-1}n\Lambda^{-1}
.

Moreover, on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
we can write

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}\right\|\mu_{n}({\boldsymbol{A}})^{-2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{B}\left(\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}+n\lambda_{k+1}^{2}\right)\cdot L^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\cdot c_{B}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{B}^{2}L^{2}}{c^{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2},

where in the last line we used the assumption from Equation (
55
).

Combining all that with Lemma
51
gives that conditionally on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})\cap\mathscr{C}
for any
t\in(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{3})
with probability at least
c_{y}^{-1}-c_{y}e^{-t^{2}/c_{y}}
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
with probability at least
0.25
over the draw of
(\varepsilon_{y},\varepsilon_{q})

\displaystyle\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\right\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}({{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\left(\left\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0:k}){\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{{0:k}}}^{2}+0.5\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{7}{2}({{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{k:\infty}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\right\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 7(\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\|{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c_{1}c_{y}}V

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c_{3}^{2}}n^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\cdot\left(c_{1}^{-1}\Lambda^{2}n^{-2}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+0.5\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{7}{2}n^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\cdot\frac{c_{B}^{2}L^{2}}{c^{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}-7c_{y}^{2}c_{2}^{2}t^{2}\Diamond^{2}\cdot c_{y}c_{2}(V+t^{2}\Delta V).

If
c
is large enough, namely if
7c_{B}^{2}L^{2}c_{3}^{2}<0.25c^{2}
, then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c_{3}^{2}}n^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\cdot\left(c_{1}^{-1}\Lambda^{2}n^{-2}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+0.5\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{7}{2}n^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\cdot\frac{c_{B}^{2}L^{2}}{c^{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c_{3}^{2}}\cdot\left(c_{1}^{-1}\left\|\left(\Lambda n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+0.25n^{2}\Lambda^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\min(c_{1}^{-1},0.25)}{2c_{3}^{2}}n\Diamond^{2}/2.

That is, for a large enough constant
c_{4}
that only depends on
\sigma_{x},c_{B},L
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle c_{4}^{-1}(V+n\Diamond^{2})-c_{4}t^{2}\Diamond^{2}(V+t^{2}\Delta V).

By Lemma
7

V\leq 2
and
t^{2}\Delta V\leq 3t^{2}/n\leq 3
. Thus,

\|\bar{S}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\geq c_{4}^{-1}(V+n\Diamond^{2}(1-5c_{4}^{2}t^{2}/n))\geq c_{5}^{-1}(V+n\Diamond^{2}),

provided that
c_{5}
is a large enough constant depending on
c_{4}
, and
t=\sqrt{n/c_{5}}
.

∎

H.3
The ratio

Finally we can put the bound on the numerator together with the bound on the denominator and obtain the following
See
9

Proof.

First of all, it is enough to show the statement for
{\bar{{\boldsymbol{w}}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}
(as defined in the beginning of Section
H
) instead of
{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}
as it has the same distribution.

The straightforward combination of Lemmas
54
and
50
almost does the job, but we also need to show that
\bar{S}>0
with high probability.

Recall that

\bar{S}=(1+\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}.

By Lemma
47
, if
c
is large enough depending on
\sigma_{x},L
, then on the event
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
we have

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}\bar{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}_{\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\perp}}}{{\vphantom{.}\smash{\scriptscriptstyle\sim}}}}}\geq M/c>0.

Moreover,
(1+\bar{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}})^{2}\geq 0
almost surely, and
\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{y}}}>0
on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)
. Thus, if
c
is large enough, then
\bar{S}>0
on
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
.

Recall that since the data is sub-Gaussian, we can take
c_{B}
in the definition of the event
\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
large enough depending only on
\sigma_{x}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B}))\geq 1-c_{B}e^{-n/c_{B}}
(as shown in Section
3.2
).
Now the first part of the theorem is a direct consequence of part 2 of Lemma
50
, while the second part of the theorem is a direct combination of Lemma
54
with the first part of
50
.
∎

See
11

Proof.

First of all, denote the constants from Theorem
9
as
a_{u},c_{u},c_{y,u}
(here index
u
stands for “upper bound”). Note that by that theorem, regardless whether
n\Lambda^{-1}M<a^{-1}\Diamond
or
n\Lambda^{-1}M\geq a^{-1}\Diamond
it still holds for any
t_{u}\in(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{y,u})
that the probability of the event

\left\{\frac{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}}{\|{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\leq c_{u}(1+t_{u})\frac{n\Lambda^{-1}M}{\sqrt{V+n\Diamond^{2}}}\right\}

is a least

(c_{y,u}^{-1}-c_{y_{u}}e^{-t_{u}^{2}/c_{y,u}}-c_{y,u}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{A}_{k}(L))-c_{u}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}.

Thus, if
t_{u},n,\delta
and
\varepsilon
are such that

(c_{y,u}^{-1}-c_{y,u}e^{-t_{u}^{2}/c_{y,u}}-c_{y}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}(1-\delta-c_{u}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}>\varepsilon,

then

\alpha_{\varepsilon}<c_{u}(1+t_{u})\frac{n\Lambda^{-1}M}{\sqrt{V+n\Diamond^{2}}}.

When it comes to the lower bound, recall that by Lemma
5
, the event
\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
holds with probability at least
1-c_{B}e^{-n/c_{B}}
for a constant
c_{B}
that only depends on
\sigma_{x}
. Thus, Theorem
8
is applicable. Denote the constant from Theorem
8
as
c_{\ell}
(here index
\ell
stands for “lower bound”). Then we have that for the case
\eta=0
(i.e.
{\boldsymbol{y}}=\hat{{\boldsymbol{y}}}
) for any
t_{\ell}\in(0,\sqrt{n}/c_{\ell})
, conditionally on the event
\mathscr{A}_{k}(L)\cap\mathscr{B}_{k}(c_{B})
, with probability at least
1-c_{\ell}e^{-t_{\ell}^{2}/2}
over the draw of
{\boldsymbol{y}}

\frac{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\top}{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}}{\|{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{\text{\tiny{ridge}}}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\geq c_{\ell}^{-2}\frac{N-c_{\ell}^{2}t_{\ell}\Diamond}{\sqrt{V+t_{\ell}^{2}\Delta V}+\Diamond\sqrt{n}}\geq c_{\ell}^{-2}\frac{N/2}{\sqrt{V(1+4t_{\ell}^{2})}+\Diamond\sqrt{n}},

where the last transition is made under the assumption that
c_{\ell}^{2}t_{\ell}\Diamond\leq N
, and also uses the fact that
\Delta V\leq 4V
(Lemma
7
).

Thus, if we take
a=2c_{\ell}^{2}t_{\ell}
and
t_{\ell},n,\varepsilon,\delta
are such that

(1-\delta-c_{B}e^{-n/c_{B}})_{+}(1-c_{\ell}e^{-t_{\ell}^{2}/2})_{+}\geq 1-\varepsilon,

then under the condition
n\Lambda^{-1}M\geq a\Diamond
we get

\alpha_{\varepsilon}\geq c_{\ell}^{-2}\frac{n\Lambda^{-1}M/2}{\sqrt{V(1+4t_{\ell}^{2})}+\Diamond\sqrt{n}}.

Finally, to finish the proof we just need to choose
c_{1}
,
t_{l}
and
t_{u}
that can only depend on
L,\sigma_{x}
and absolute constants
\delta,\varepsilon
such that for any
n>c_{1}

\displaystyle(1-\delta-c_{B}e^{-n/c_{B}})_{+}(1-c_{y,\ell}e^{-t_{\ell}^{2}/2})_{+}>

\displaystyle 1-\varepsilon,

\displaystyle(c_{y,u}^{-1}-c_{y,u}e^{-t_{u}^{2}/c_{y,u}}-c_{y}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}(1-\delta-c_{u}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}>

\displaystyle\varepsilon.

This is easy to do: first choose
t_{u}
large enough so that
c_{y_{u}}e^{-t_{u}^{2}/c_{y,u}}<c_{y_{u}}^{-1}/2
. Note that
t_{u}
is an absolute constant. Second, take
\varepsilon=0.5\wedge(c_{y_{u}}^{-1}/16)
— an absolute constant. Third, take
c_{1}
large enough depending on
c_{B},c_{y,u},c_{u},\varepsilon
so that

c_{y}e^{-c_{1}/c_{u}}\leq c_{y,u}^{-1}/4,\quad c_{y}e^{-c_{1}/c_{u}}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad c_{B}e^{-c_{1}/c_{B}}\leq\varepsilon/4.

Fourth, take
\delta=\varepsilon/4
– an absolute constant. Finally, take
t_{\ell}
such that
c_{\ell}e^{-t_{\ell}^{2}/2}\leq\varepsilon/2
— a constant that only depends on
c_{\ell}
(which, in its turn, only depends on
L
and
\sigma_{x}
). Combining all gives

\displaystyle(1-\delta-c_{B}e^{-n/c_{B}})_{+}(1-c_{y,\ell}e^{-t_{\ell}^{2}/2})_{+}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon/4-\varepsilon/4)_{+}(1-\varepsilon/2)_{+}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 1-\varepsilon,

\displaystyle(c_{y,u}^{-1}-c_{y,u}e^{-t_{u}^{2}/c_{y,u}}-c_{y}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}(1-\delta-c_{u}e^{-n/c_{u}})_{+}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle(c_{y,u}^{-1}-c_{y,u}^{-1}/2-c_{y,u}^{-1}/4)(1-1/4-1/4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{y_{u}}^{-1}/16\geq\varepsilon,

which finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix I
Analysis of ridge regularization

See
15

Proof.

The idea is to introduce the vector-valued function
{\boldsymbol{w}}(t):=({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{v}}
and to compute the derivative of
f\left({\boldsymbol{w}}(t)\right)
in
t
using the chain rule as

\frac{d}{dt}f\left({\boldsymbol{w}}(t)\right)=\left(\frac{d}{dt}{\boldsymbol{w}}(t)\right)^{\top}\left(\nabla f({\boldsymbol{w}})\big|_{{\boldsymbol{w}}={\boldsymbol{w}}(t)}\right).

We write

\displaystyle f({\boldsymbol{w}}):=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{w}}/\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|,

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}f({\boldsymbol{w}})=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{v}}/\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|-{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{w}}/\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|^{3},

\displaystyle\dot{{\boldsymbol{w}}}:=\frac{d}{dt}{{\boldsymbol{w}}}=

\displaystyle-{\boldsymbol{M}}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-t^{-1}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}}-{\boldsymbol{I}}_{p})({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-t^{-1}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{v}}+t^{-1}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-t^{-1}\left({\boldsymbol{w}}+({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{w}}\right).

\displaystyle t{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\dot{{\boldsymbol{w}}}=

\displaystyle-{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{w}}+\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|^{2},

\displaystyle t{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\top}\dot{{\boldsymbol{w}}}=

\displaystyle-\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|^{2}+{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{w}},

\displaystyle t\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|^{3}\langle\nabla f({\boldsymbol{w}}),\dot{{\boldsymbol{w}}}\rangle=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|^{4}-{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{w}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{w}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{v}},({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-3/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle-\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}\cdot\|({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{M}})^{-3/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 0,

where the last transition is by Cauchy-Schwartz and we used the brackets
\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}\rangle
to denote the scalar product
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}
. We see that the derivative of
f({\boldsymbol{w}}(t))
is non-positive when
t>0
, thus the function
f({\boldsymbol{w}}(t))
is non-increasing in
t
on
[0,+\infty)
.
∎

See
16

Proof.

First of all, note that
\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})>\Lambda(\lambda)>n\lambda_{k}
. Thus, by Lemma
14
we can show that for some absolute constant
c_{1}>0

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}\vee\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}\leq c_{1}\left(1+\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\vee\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\right),

where we denoted
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda(\lambda)
,
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})
and used the notation from Lemma
14
.

From now on we forget about the notion of
k
and only study the following quantity as a function of
\Lambda
:

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda)}.

Note that if we denote

\displaystyle t:=\Lambda/n,\quad{\boldsymbol{v}}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\quad{\boldsymbol{w}}:=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+t{\boldsymbol{I}}_{p})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=({\boldsymbol{I}}_{p}+t{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{v}},

then it becomes

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda)}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{w}}}{\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\|}.

Thus, by Lemma
15
,
\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda)}
is a non-increasing function of
\Lambda
, i.e., the benefit of regularization could only potentially come from the term
\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{V(\Lambda)}}
. More precisely, suppose that

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}<\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}.

Then
\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\geq\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})
, otherwise we would have

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}=\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}.

Moreover, if
\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}<\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})
then by Intermediate Value Theorem we can take such
\Lambda_{0.5}
that
\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}=\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})
, and we’ll once again have

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}=\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0.5})}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}.

In case
\Lambda_{0.5}
as above exists set
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0.5}
, otherwise set
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}
. Now we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}\geq\Lambda>\Lambda_{0},

\displaystyle\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}\geq\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda),\quad\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\geq\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0}),

\displaystyle\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}<\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda)}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}.

Let’s study
\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}
. The idea is to re-introduce
k
, but the “right one” (basically choose
k=k^{*}
). Then split into the
{0:k}
and
{k:\infty}
part and say that increasing regularization does nothing to the tail, but also cannot make the
{0:k}
part more than a constant. Formally, take
k_{0}=\min\{\kappa:\lambda_{\kappa+1}<\Lambda_{0}/n\}
. Such
k_{0}<n
exists since
\Lambda_{0}>n\lambda_{k+1}
.

Now we write

\displaystyle\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}+\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}+\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}+\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\mu_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda+1)^{2}}}}

The second term is a decreasing function of
\Lambda
, which implies

\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\mu_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda+1)^{2}}}}\leq\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\mu_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda_{0}+1)^{2}}}}=\frac{\sum_{i>k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\Lambda_{0}/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}}\leq 2\frac{\sum_{i}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}}=2\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}},

where we used that
(\Lambda_{0}/n)^{-1}\leq 2(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{-1}
for
i>k_{0}
in the last inequality.

Now let’s study the part that comes from the first
k_{0}
components. We can write

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}

By Lemma
15
, the first multiplier is a non-increasing function of
\Lambda
. Moreover, if we plug
\Lambda_{0}
instead of
\Lambda
we get

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}}\leq 2\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}}=2\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda_{0}/n)^{2}}}\\
\leq 2\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})\leq 2\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\leq 2\sqrt{2},

where we used that
\lambda_{i}\geq\Lambda_{0}/n
for
i\leq k_{0}
. In the last transition we also used that
{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})<V(\Lambda_{0})
(Lemma
14
) and
V(\Lambda_{0})<2
(Lemma
7
).

Thus, the first multiplier starts less than a constant and stays less than a constant. For the second multiplier we have

\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{2}/n}{(\lambda_{i}+\Lambda/n)^{2}}}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}\leq 1.

Overall, we’ve got that either

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}\geq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})},

or

\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{1})}\leq\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda)}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda)}}\leq 2\sqrt{2}+2\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}=2\sqrt{2}+2\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{{V_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}}\wedge\frac{{N_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\Lambda_{0})},

which implies the desired result.

∎

See
18

Proof.

Denote the constants from Theorem
11
as
a_{0},c_{0},\delta_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
.

First of all, let’s show that if
a
is chosen to be equal to
a_{0}
, then for any
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda
it holds
n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})\geq a_{0}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})
. Indeed, if
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|=0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}}

— doesn’t depend on
\lambda^{\prime}
at all.

In the case that
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is an eigenvector of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, that is,
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mu_{i}{\boldsymbol{e}}_{i}
for some
i\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(1+\lambda_{i}^{-1}n^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})\right)^{-1}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mu_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}\left(1+\lambda_{i}^{-1}n^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})\right)^{-1}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mu_{i}^{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{n/\lambda_{i}}

— doesn’t depend on
\lambda^{\prime}
once again.

That is

\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}=\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\Diamond(\lambda)}\geq a=a_{0}.

Note also that

\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})\geq\Lambda(\lambda)>c\left(n\lambda_{k+1}\vee\sqrt{n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}}\right),

and

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{A}_{k}(L,\lambda^{\prime}))\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{A}_{k}(L,\lambda))\geq 1-\delta_{0},

so if
c>c_{0}
,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta=\delta_{0}
then the assumptions of Theorem
11
are satisfied for both
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
, which yields

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)/c_{0}\leq\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{V(\lambda)}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\leq c_{0}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda),\quad\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime})/c_{0}\leq\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{V(\lambda^{\prime})}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\leq c_{0}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime}).

We’ve already seen that
\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}=\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}
, so the only thing we need to study is
V(\lambda)
. Namely, we are going to show that under the assumptions we made
V(\lambda)<n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)
and
V(\lambda^{\prime})<n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda^{\prime})
. That will finish the proof since in that case

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime})/c_{0}\leq\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{V(\lambda^{\prime})}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\leq\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\\
=\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\leq 2\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{V(\lambda^{\prime})}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\leq 2c_{0}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda),

and analogously
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)/c_{0}\leq 2c_{0}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime})
.

Thus, in the rest of the proof we show that
V(\lambda)<n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)
and
V(\lambda^{\prime})<n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda^{\prime})
.
Let’s write out two cases:

1.

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is supported on the tail. Then

\displaystyle V(\lambda):=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle\Diamond^{2}:=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

We want to show that

n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+n^{-1}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2},

which holds since

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+n^{-1}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}\right)^{-2}\right)+n^{-1}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}.

We showed that
V(\lambda)\leq n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)
. Note that
V(\lambda^{\prime})\leq n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda^{\prime})
by exactly the same argument.

2.

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{e}}_{i}
for
i\leq k
. Write out
V
and
\Diamond
again:

\displaystyle V(\lambda):=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle\Diamond(\lambda)^{2}:=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\frac{\lambda_{i}^{-1}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(n^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda)\lambda_{i}^{-1}+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(\Lambda(\lambda)+n\lambda_{i})^{2}}

By the same argument as before, since

\frac{n\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(1+n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda(\lambda))^{2}}\geq\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2},

we have
V(\lambda)<n\Diamond(\lambda)^{2}
. When it comes to
\lambda^{\prime}
, we can write

\frac{n\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(1+n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime}))^{2}}\geq\frac{n\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}}{(1+n\lambda_{i}/\Lambda(\lambda))^{2}}\geq\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2},

which yields
V(\lambda^{\prime})<n\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})^{2}.

∎

See
19

Proof.

Take
a,\varepsilon,\delta
to be the same as in Theorem
11
. Denote the constant
c
from that theorem as
c_{1}
. In the end we will take
c
large enough depending on
c_{1}
. If
c>c_{1}
then Theorem
11
implies that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{V(\lambda)}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{2\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}.

At the same time, by Theorem
11
for any
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime})\leq c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{V(\lambda^{\prime})}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\leq c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}.

Take such
\hat{\lambda}
that
\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})=C\Lambda(\lambda)
. Note that
\hat{\lambda}>\lambda
. By our construction, due to the fact that
\lambda_{k}>C\Lambda(\lambda)=\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})
and Equation (
29
) we have

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)\geq

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2},

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}M(\hat{\lambda})\leq

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2}+n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2},

\displaystyle n\Diamond(\lambda)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2}+n^{2}\Lambda({\lambda})^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2},

\displaystyle n\Diamond(\hat{\lambda})^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2},

where we used that
\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal elements are at most
2
in the last transition.

Combining everything together we get that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{2c_{1}}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}}=\\
=\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}}\frac{2n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}}\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}M(\hat{\lambda})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\hat{\lambda})}\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\lambda}).

We obtained the result for
\lambda^{\prime}=\hat{\lambda}
. To extend the result for all
\lambda^{\prime}>\hat{\lambda}
note that by Lemma
14
and the fact that
{N_{a}}(\lambda)/{\Diamond_{a}}(\lambda)
is a non-increasing function of
\lambda
for some absolute constant
c_{2}>1
we can write

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\hat{\lambda})^{-1}M(\hat{\lambda})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\hat{\lambda})}\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}c_{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{{N_{a}}(\hat{\lambda})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}(\hat{\lambda})}\geq\\
\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}c_{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{{N_{a}}({\lambda^{\prime}})}{\sqrt{n}{\Diamond_{a}}({\lambda^{\prime}})}\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda({\lambda^{\prime}})^{-1}M({\lambda^{\prime}})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond({\lambda^{\prime}})}\geq\frac{C}{8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}}\alpha_{\varepsilon}({\lambda^{\prime}}).

Taking
c=8\sqrt{2}c_{1}^{2}c_{2}^{2}
finishes the proof.
∎

See
20

Proof.

Since we consider Gaussian data,
\sigma_{x}
is an absolute constant.
Let’s take
L=2
and denote the corresponding constant
c
from Lemma
19
to be
c_{1}
. We are going to use that lemma to construct such distribution of data that the quantile
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)
is minimized for a negative
\lambda
. Note that to do that it is enough to take
C=c_{1}
and
\lambda=-\frac{c_{1}-1}{c_{1}}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}
as this condition is equivalent to
\Lambda(0)=c_{1}\Lambda(\lambda)
.

Let’s take infinite-dimensional Gaussian data with slow exponential decay in the tail, that is

\lambda_{i}=\begin{cases}\ell,&i\leq k,\\
e^{-\alpha(i-k)},&i>k.\end{cases},\quad\mu_{i}=\begin{cases}m_{0:k},&i\leq k,\\
m_{k:\infty}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(i-k)},&i>k.\end{cases}

Thus, the whole classification problem is described by scalars
\ell,\alpha,\beta,m_{0:k},m_{k:\infty},n,k
.

Let’s see how we need to choose those scalars in order to follow the recipe from Lemma
19
. This is an absolute constant since
L
is an absolute constant and the data is Gaussian. As discussed before, we fix
C=c_{1}
and put
\lambda=-\frac{c_{1}-1}{c_{1}}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}

Due to Lemma
3
, for Gaussian data the statement
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{A}_{k}(L,\lambda))\geq 1-\delta
follows from the statement

\Lambda(\lambda)\geq b\left(n\lambda_{k+1}\vee\sqrt{n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}}\right),

where
b
is a large constant that depends on
\delta
. Let’s also take it to be larger than
c_{1}
in order to fully satisfy step 1 of Lemma
19
. For our covariance and regularization this translates into

\frac{1}{c_{1}}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\geq b\left(ne^{-\alpha}\vee\sqrt{n\frac{e^{-2\alpha}}{1-e^{-2\alpha}}}\right),

which can be equivalently rewritten as

1-e^{-\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{bc_{1}\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\wedge\sqrt{1-e^{-2\alpha}}\right).

For
x\in(0,1)
it holds that
1-x<e^{-x}<1-x(1-e^{-1})<1-x/2.
Thus, assuming that
\alpha<0.5
the condition above follows from the following:

\alpha\leq\frac{1}{bc_{1}\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\wedge\sqrt{\alpha}\right),

that is

\alpha\leq\frac{1}{bc_{1}n}\wedge\frac{1}{b^{2}c_{1}^{2}n}=\frac{1}{b^{2}c_{1}^{2}n}.

Let’s take
c_{2}=b^{2}c_{1}^{2}
and put
\alpha=c_{2}^{-1}n^{-1}
. Then conditions from step 1 of Lemma
19
are satisfied.

The second part of Lemma
19
states that we require
n\lambda_{k}\geq C\Lambda(\lambda)
, that is,
n\ell\geq C\left(\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}+\lambda\right)=\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}
. Note that we also need
\ell\geq 1
in order for the sequence
\{\lambda_{i}\}
to be non-increasing. Given our previous choice of
\alpha
we have

\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}=2c_{2}n.

Thus, we can take
\ell=2c_{2}
.

The third part of Lemma
19
requires the following

\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}\leq C^{-1}n^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2},

which we equivalently transform below:

\displaystyle\sum_{i>k}e^{-(\alpha+\beta)(i-k)}\leq C^{-2}n^{-1}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\sum_{i>k}e^{-\beta(i-k)},

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}{1-e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}\leq C^{-2}n^{-1}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\frac{e^{-\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}},

\displaystyle\frac{1-e^{-\beta}}{1-e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}\leq C^{-2}n^{-1}\frac{1}{1-e^{-\alpha}}.

Let’s restrict the range of
\beta
so that
\alpha+\beta<1
. Then it is sufficient to choose
\beta
that satisfies the following stronger condition:

\frac{2\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\leq\frac{1}{C^{2}n\alpha}.

Plugging the expression for
\alpha
yields

2\beta\leq\frac{c_{2}}{C^{2}}\left(\beta+\frac{1}{c_{2}n}\right).

Actually, since
c_{2}=b^{2}C^{2}>2C^{2}
, the inequality above always holds, so we can take any
\beta<1+\alpha
, for example,
\beta=\ln(2)
(to make further computations simpler).

Next, part 4 of Lemma
19
requires

nC^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}\geq\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2}\geq n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2},

that is,

n\frac{1-e^{-\alpha}}{e^{-\alpha}}\frac{e^{-\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}\geq\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell m_{k:\infty}^{2}}\geq n^{2}C^{2}\frac{(1-e^{-\alpha})^{2}}{e^{-2\alpha}}\frac{e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}{1-e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}.

Plugging in
\beta=\ln(2)
and simplifying yields

n\frac{1-e^{-\alpha}}{e^{-\alpha}}\geq\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell m_{k:\infty}^{2}}\geq n^{2}C^{2}\frac{(1-e^{-\alpha})^{2}}{e^{-\alpha}}\frac{1}{2-e^{-\alpha}}.

As before, let’s replace it by a stronger condition:

\frac{n\alpha}{2}\geq\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell m_{k:\infty}^{2}}\geq 2n^{2}C^{2}\alpha^{2}.

Plugging in
\alpha=(c_{2}n)^{-1}
and
\ell=2c_{2}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2c_{2}}\geq\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}\geq\frac{2C^{2}}{c_{2}^{2}},

\displaystyle 1\geq\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{m_{k:\infty}^{2}}\geq\frac{4C^{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{4}{b^{2}}.

We see that since
b>4
, we can simply put
km_{0:k}^{2}/m_{k:\infty}^{2}=1
, and part 4 of Lemma
19
is satisfied.

At last, we need to check the last part of the lemma. Let’s start with writing out and transforming the expressions for
n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda),V(\lambda),
and
n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\Lambda(\lambda)=

\displaystyle C^{-1}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\in\left(\frac{1}{2C\alpha},\frac{2}{C\alpha}\right),

\displaystyle V(\lambda):=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\frac{k}{\left(1+n^{-1}\ell^{-1}C^{-1}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\right)^{2}}+C^{2}ne^{2\alpha}(1-e^{-\alpha})^{2}\frac{e^{-2\alpha}}{1-e^{-2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{k/n}{\left(1+n^{-1}\ell^{-1}C^{-1}/(2\alpha)\right)^{2}}+C^{2}n\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k/n}{\left(1+n^{-1}(2c_{2})^{-1}C^{-1}c_{2}n/2\right)^{2}}+C^{2}n\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k/n}{\left(1+0.25C^{-1}\right)^{2}}+C^{2}c_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2.

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}(\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k\frac{m_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell(1+n^{-1}\ell^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda))}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}\frac{e^{-\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle k\frac{m_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell(1+2n^{-1}\ell^{-1}/(C\alpha))}+\frac{1}{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}nC\alpha(\lambda)\frac{e^{-\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{km_{0:k}^{2}}{2c_{2}+2c_{2}/C}+\frac{1}{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}nC/(c_{2}n)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4c_{2}}(km_{0:k}^{2}+m_{k:\infty}^{2})=\frac{m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{2c_{2}}.

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k\frac{m_{0:k}^{2}}{\ell(1+n^{-1}\ell^{-1}\Lambda(\lambda))^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\frac{e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}{1-e^{-(\alpha+\beta)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{(2c_{2}+\Lambda(\lambda)/n)^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{2-e^{-\alpha}}

For
n\Diamond^{2}
we need bounds from both sides. In what follows we write them separately.

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{(2c_{2}+0.5n^{-1}C^{-1}\alpha^{-1})^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}n^{2}(2C\alpha)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{(2c_{2}+0.5c_{2}C^{-1})^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}(2C/c_{2})^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 5m_{k:\infty}^{2}/c_{2},

where in the last transition we used that
c_{2}/C=b>\sqrt{c_{2}}
.

When it comes to the bound from below, we write

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{(2c_{2}+2n^{-1}C^{-1}\alpha^{-1})^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}n^{2}(2C\alpha)^{2}/3

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{2}m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{(2c_{2}+2c_{2}C^{-1})^{2}}+m_{k:\infty}^{2}(2C/c_{2})^{2}/3

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle m_{k:\infty}^{2}/(8c_{2}),

where we used
2c_{2}+2c_{2}C^{-1}\leq 4c_{2}
in the last transition.

Finally, we can write out the conditions from part 5 of Lemma
19
. According to the bounds above, the following conditions on
m_{k:\infty}
are sufficient:

m_{k:\infty}^{2}/(8c_{2})\geq 2,\quad\frac{m_{k:\infty}^{2}}{2c_{2}}\geq a\sqrt{\frac{5}{c_{2}n}}m_{k:\infty},

that is
m_{k:\infty}^{2}\geq(16c_{2})\vee(5c_{2}a^{2}/n)=16c_{2}
given that
n
is large enough.
∎

See
21

Proof.

Take
a,\varepsilon,\delta
to be the same as in Theorem
11
. Denote the constant
c
from that theorem as
c_{1}
. In the end we will take
c
large enough depending on
c_{1}
. If
c>c_{1}
then Theorem
11
implies that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{V(\lambda)}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{2\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}.

At the same time, by Theorem
11
for any
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime})\leq c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{V(\lambda^{\prime})}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\leq c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}.

Since
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|=0
,
\Lambda(\lambda)\leq n\lambda_{k}
and
\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})>n\lambda_{1}
, we can write

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2},

\displaystyle n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}\right)^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|^{2},

\displaystyle n\Diamond(\lambda)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2},

\displaystyle n\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}^{2},

where we used the fact that
\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}
and
\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}
are both diagonal matrices whose diagonal elements are at most
2
.

Combining everything together we get that

\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\geq c_{1}^{-1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}M(\lambda)}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{2c_{1}}\frac{\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}}=\frac{1}{4c_{1}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}\geq\frac{C}{4c_{1}}\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}}=\\
=\frac{C}{16c_{1}}\frac{2n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}}\geq\frac{C}{16c_{1}^{2}}\cdot c_{1}\frac{n\Lambda(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}M(\lambda^{\prime})}{\sqrt{n}\Diamond(\lambda^{\prime})}\geq\frac{C}{16c_{1}^{2}}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda^{\prime}).

Taking
c=16c_{1}^{2}
finishes the proof.
∎

See
23

Proof.

Since we consider Gaussian data,
\sigma_{x}
is an absolute constant.
Let’s take
L=2
and denote the corresponding constants
a,c
from Lemma
21
to be
a_{1},c_{1}
. We are going to use that lemma to construct such distribution of data that the quantile
\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)
is minimized for a negative
\lambda
. Note that to do that it is enough to take
C=c_{1}
and
\lambda=-\frac{c_{1}-1}{c_{1}}\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}
as this condition is equivalent to
\Lambda(0)=c_{1}\Lambda(\lambda)
.

Let’s take finite-dimensional Gaussian data with exponential decay in the first
k
components and isotropic tails:

\lambda_{i}=\begin{cases}e^{-\alpha i},&i\leq k,\\
\ell,&i>k.\end{cases}

Note that
\Lambda(\lambda)=\Lambda(0)/c_{1}=\ell(p-k)/c_{1}
.

We take
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}
to be zero, in accordance with Lemma
21
. When it comes to
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}
, we just put all its components to be equal, that is

\mu_{i}=\begin{cases}m,&i\leq k,\\
0,&i>k.\end{cases}

Thus, the whole classification problem is described by scalars
\ell,m,\alpha,n,k,p
.

Our goal is to find the values of these parameters such that the conditions from Lemma
21
are satisfied for some
L
. We take
L=2
.

The first part of that lemma says that
\mathscr{A}_{k}(2)
should be satisfied with probability at least
1-\delta
and that

\ell(p-k)/c_{1}=\Lambda(\lambda)\geq c_{1}\left(n\lambda_{k+1}\vee\sqrt{n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}}\right)=c_{1}\ell(n\vee\sqrt{n(p-k)}).

Due to Lemma
3
, for Gaussian data both these conditions follow from
p>bn
and
n>b
, where
b
is a large enough absolute constant.

The second part of Lemma
21
requires
n\lambda_{i}>\Lambda(\lambda)
, that is,
ne^{-\alpha k}\geq\ell(p-k)/c_{1}
, so we can take
\ell=c_{1}ne^{-\alpha k}/p
. Note that since
p>c_{1}n
we have
\lambda_{k+1}=\ell<e^{-\alpha k}=\lambda_{k}
, so the eigenvalues remain in the right order.

The third part of Lemma
21
demands
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}}\geq C\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\|^{2}
, that is,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\left(m^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{-\alpha i}\right)\left(m^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}\right)}\geq c_{1}km^{2},

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1-e^{-k\alpha}}{1-e^{-\alpha}}\frac{e^{k\alpha}-1}{e^{\alpha}-1}}\geq c_{1}k.

Note that for
\alpha>0
we have
1-e^{-k\alpha}>1-e^{-\alpha}
. Moreover,
e^{k\alpha}-1>(e^{\alpha}-1)e^{(k-1)\alpha}
. Thus, it is enough to satisfy the following weaker condition:

e^{(k-1)\alpha/2}\geq c_{1}k,

so we need to take
\alpha\geq 2\ln(c_{1}k)/(k-1)
. Since
k
is lower bounded by a large constant
b
, we can take
\alpha=4\ln(k)/k
.
Plugging it into equation for
\ell
yields
\ell=c_{1}ne^{-\alpha k}/(ep)=c_{1}n/(epk^{4})
. We take
c=c_{1}/e
, so
\ell=cnp^{-1}k^{-4}
.

Finally, we need to take
m
large enough so that part 4 of Lemma
21
is satisfied. To check that part, we start with writing the expressions for
n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda),V(\lambda),
and
n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda)
and bounding them.

\displaystyle\Lambda(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\ell(p-k)/c_{1}=\frac{n(p-k)}{epk^{4}},

\displaystyle V(\lambda):=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\text{tr}\left(\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-2}\right)+\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{k}{n}+\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k}{n}+\frac{n}{p-k}.

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n\Lambda(\lambda)^{-1}(\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{e^{\alpha i}}{1+e^{\alpha i}(p-k)/(epk^{4})}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{1+e^{\alpha k}/(ek^{4})}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{1+e^{-1}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}.

Let’s bound the sum of exponents separately. We write

\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}=e^{\alpha}\frac{e^{\alpha k}-1}{e^{\alpha}-1}=\frac{k^{4}-1}{1-e^{-4\ln(k)/k}}\begin{cases}\geq\frac{k^{5}}{5\ln(k)}\\
\leq\frac{k^{5}}{2\ln(k)}\end{cases}

where we used that
\alpha<1
(since
k
is large enough) and for
x\in(0,1)
it holds
x>1-e^{-x}>x/2
.

Thus,

n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda)\geq\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{10\ln(k)}.

When it comes to
\Diamond
, the derivation is very similar as above:

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda):=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda(\lambda)n^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1}+{\boldsymbol{I}}_{k}\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0:k}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0:k}\right\|^{2}+n^{2}\Lambda(\lambda)^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k:\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{e^{\alpha i}}{\left(1+e^{\alpha i}(p-k)/(epk^{4})\right)^{2}}

For
n\Diamond^{2}
we need bounds from both sides. In what follows we write them separately.

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)\leq

\displaystyle m^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{e^{\alpha i}}{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{2\ln(k)},

\displaystyle n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{\left(1+e^{\alpha k}/(ek^{4})\right)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{(1+e^{-1})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{\alpha i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{20\ln(k)}.

Finally, to satisfy part 4 of Lemma
21
we need

n\Diamond^{2}(\lambda)\geq V(\lambda),\quad n\Lambda^{-1}(\lambda)M(\lambda)\geq a_{1}\Diamond(\lambda),

that is, it is enough to have

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{20\ln(k)}\geq\frac{k}{n}+\frac{n}{p-k},\quad\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{10\ln(k)}\geq a_{1}\sqrt{\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{2n\ln(k)}},

which is equivalent to

\frac{m^{2}k^{5}}{20\ln(k)}\geq\left(\frac{k}{n}+\frac{n}{p-k}\right)\vee\frac{50a_{1}}{n}.

For example, we can put

m=\frac{b\ln(k)}{k^{5}}\left(\frac{k}{n}+\frac{n}{p}\right)

given that
b
is a large enough constant.
∎

Appendix J
Comparisons with earlier results

See
30

Proof.

First of all, due to assumptions on
\lambda_{i}
,
\lambda
and
k
we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\Lambda,

\displaystyle\Lambda=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\in[\kappa p,p],

\displaystyle V=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}\leq n/\Lambda\leq\frac{n}{\kappa p},

\displaystyle\Delta V\leq

\displaystyle\frac{n\lambda_{1}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}+\frac{n\lambda_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\leq\frac{2n}{\kappa^{2}p^{2}}+\frac{1}{\kappa p}\leq\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}p},

\displaystyle\Diamond^{2}=

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\leq\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}}{\kappa^{2}p^{2}},

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}.

Plugging in those bounds together with
\sigma_{\eta}<1
yields

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-1}M-ct\Diamond\geq

\displaystyle\frac{n}{p}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}-ct\frac{\sqrt{n}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|}{\kappa{p}},

\displaystyle\left[1+n\Lambda^{-1}M\sigma_{\eta}\right]\sqrt{V+t^{2}\Delta V}+\Diamond\sqrt{n}\leq

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{n}{\kappa p}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}\right]\sqrt{\frac{n}{\kappa p}+t^{2}\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}p}}+\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|}{\kappa{p}}.

Next, since
n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}/p\leq\kappa
for
t^{2}\leq n\kappa
we can write

\displaystyle\left[1+n\Lambda^{-1}M\sigma_{\eta}\right]\sqrt{V+t^{2}\Delta V}+\Diamond\sqrt{n}\leq

\displaystyle[1+1]\sqrt{\frac{4n}{\kappa p}}+\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|}{\kappa{p}}\leq 5\sqrt{\frac{n}{p\kappa}}.

Finally, if
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|\geq 2ct/(\kappa\sqrt{n})
, then
\frac{n}{p}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}-ct\frac{\sqrt{n}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|}{\kappa{p}}\geq\frac{n}{2p}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}
. Plugging in that bound in yields the result.
∎

See
25

Proof.

Let’s write out the definitions of
\Lambda,M,\Diamond,V,\Delta V
for the case
k=0
with
n\lambda_{1}<\lambda+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
:

\displaystyle\Lambda=

\displaystyle\lambda+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=\lambda+\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}),

\displaystyle V=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-2}n\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}^{2}=\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}^{2}}{\left(\lambda+\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle\Delta V=

\displaystyle\frac{n\lambda_{1}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}+\frac{n\lambda_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}=\frac{2n\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}^{2}}{\left(\lambda+\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle\Diamond^{2}=

\displaystyle n\Lambda^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}=\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}}{\left(\lambda+\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}.

Now we can rewrite our bound as

\displaystyle\frac{n\Lambda^{-1}M-ct\Diamond}{\sqrt{V+t^{2}\Delta V}+\sqrt{n}\Diamond}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{nM-ct\Lambda\Diamond}{\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}V+t^{2}\Lambda^{2}\Delta V}+\sqrt{n}\Lambda\Diamond}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}-ct\sqrt{n}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}}{\sqrt{n\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}^{2}+t^{2}\left(2n\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}^{2}\right)}+n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}}.

We see that for
t\leq\sqrt{n}
the condition
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|^{2}\geq 2c\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
ensures that the numerator greater or equal to
n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{k:\infty}\|^{2}/2
. At the same time, the denominator doesn’t exceed
n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\sqrt{2n}\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{F}+2n\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|
Thus, we obtain the desired result.
∎
